Regularity of cylindrical singular sets of mean curvature flow
Ao Sun, Zhihan Wang, Jinxin Xue

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the k k -cylindrical singular set of mean curvature flow in ℝ n + 1 \mathbb{R}^{n+1} for each 1 ≤ k ≤ n − 1 1\leq k\leq n-1 . We prove that they are locally contained in a k k -dimensional C 2 , α C^{2,\alpha} -submanifold after removing some lower-dimensional parts. Moreover, if the k k -cylindrical singular set is a k k -submanifold, then its curvature is determined by the asymptotic profile of the flow at these singularities. As a byproduct, we provide a detailed asymptotic profile and graphical radius estimate at these singularities. The proof is based on a new L 2 L^{2} -distance non-concentration property that we introduced in [ SWX25 ] , modified into a relative version that allows us to modulo those low eigenmodes that are not decaying fast enough and do not contribute to the curvature of the singular set.



1.
Introduction

This is the second in a series of papers to study the connections between geometry, topology, and dynamics of cylindrical singularities of mean curvature flow (MCF). In
[
SX25b
,
SWX25
]
, we observed that the dynamics of the cylindrical singularities influence the shape of mean curvature flows near the singularities, and moreover, we introduced the notion of
nondegenerate cylindrical singularities
, proved that they are isolated in spacetime, and provided a relatively complete picture about the geometry and topology change of the mean curvature flows near such singularities.
On the other hand, the famous “marriage ring” example (see Figure 17 in
[
CMP15
]
), which is the evolution of a very thin torus that collapses to a round circle as the singular set under the mean curvature flow, shows that the singular set can be a smooth curve. As we shall see later, these singularities are
degenerate
.

To state our main theorem, let us begin with some basic settings. We consider mean curvature flows in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
n\geq 2
. Throughout this paper, “mean curvature flow” is always referred to as a unit-regular
n
-dimensional Brakke flow
t\mapsto\mathbf{M}(t)
with finite entropy (see these terminologies in Section
2.2
). For readers who are not experts in geometric measure theory, one can view it as a time-dependent family of hypersurfaces satisfying the mean curvature flow equation

\partial_{t}X=\vec{H}_{\mathbf{M}(t)}(X)\,,

but the hypersurfaces can have singularities.

Given a singularity
p_{\circ}=(X_{\circ},t_{\circ})
of
\mathbf{M}
, Huisken
[
Hui90
]
introduced the “rescaled mean curvature flow (RMCF)” of
\mathbf{M}
based at
p_{\circ}
:

\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}(\tau)=e^{\tau/2}(\mathbf{M}(t_{\circ}-e^{-\tau})-X_{\circ})\,,

whose long-time subsequential (weak) limits, known as
self-shrinkers
, model the infinitesimal behavior of the flow
\mathbf{M}
near
p_{\circ}
and hence are called tangent flows of
\mathbf{M}
at
p_{\circ}
. A typical family of self-shrinkers that we focus on in this paper is the
round (generalized) cylinders
:

\mathcal{C}_{n,k}:=S^{n-k}\left(\sqrt{2(n-k)}\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}

defined for
k=1,\cdots,n-1
. We say
p_{\circ}
is a
k
-cylindrical singularity
if
\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}(\tau)
locally smoothly converges to a rotation of
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
. It was proved by
[
CM15
]
that if a subsequence
\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}(\tau_{i})
locally smoothly converges to
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, then there exist radius
R(\tau)\to+\infty
as
\tau\to+\infty
such that for
\tau\gg 1
,
\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}(\tau)
can be written as a graph of some smooth function
u(\cdot,\tau)
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
in
B_{R(\tau)}
, and that
u(\cdot,\tau)\to 0
smoothly as
\tau\to+\infty
. We call a cylindrical singularity
p_{\circ}

degenerate
if
u(\cdot,\tau)
decays exponentially in
\tau
.

Given a mean curvature flow
\mathbf{M}
, we define

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})&:=\{p:p\text{ is a $k$-cylindrical singularity}\}\,;\\
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}&:=\{p\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M}):\text{$p$ is degenerate}\}\,;\\
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}&:=\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})\backslash\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}\,.\end{split}

Our main theorem describes the regularity of the
k
-cylindrical singular sets. In the following,
\dim_{H}
denotes the space Hausdorff dimension and
\dim_{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the spacetime parabolic Hausdorff dimension in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
(see the definition in Section
2.1
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a mean curvature flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Then
\forall\,1\leq k\leq n-1
,

(i)

\dim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}\leq k-1
; moreover, when
k=1
,
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}
consists of isolated points;

(ii)

\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
is relatively closed in
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})
;

(iii)

\forall\,\alpha\in(0,\min\{1,\frac{2}{n-k}\})
,
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
is locally contained in a
k
-dimensional
C^{2,\alpha}
-submanifold, i.e. for every
p\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
, there is a neighborhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
of
p
and a
k
-dimensional
C^{2,\alpha}
-submanifold
\Gamma_{p}
properly embedded in
U
such that
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}\cap U\subset\Gamma_{p}
;

(iv)

let
\mathfrak{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
(X,t)\mapsto t
be the time function, then

\dim_{H}(\mathfrak{t}(\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}))\leq\left(3+\min\left\{1,\,\frac{2}{n-k}\right\}\right)^{-1}\cdot k\,.

In addition, if
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
is a
k
-dimensional submanifold near
p_{\circ}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
, then its second fundamental form at
p_{\circ}
is explicitly determined by the asymptotics of the graphical function of the rescaled mean curvature flow
\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}
. We will discuss more about this after we discuss the local asymptotics in Theorem
1.4
, see Remark
1.7
.

It might be a surprise to see that the singular set carries some regularity, because it does not satisfy any PDE. The proof is based on the connection between the local dynamics of the singularity and the shape of the mean curvature flow near the singularity. The key observation is that the better we know about the local dynamics of the singularity, the better the regularity of the singular set we can get.

As the singularity model, the round cylinders
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
are prevalent from the following aspects:

(1)

the round sphere
S^{n}(\sqrt{2n})
and round cylinders
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
are the only stable singularity models from the dynamic point of view; c.f.
[
CM12
,
SX21
,
SX25a
]
. Recently, with the work of Chodosh-Choi-Mantoulidis-Schulze and Chodosh-Choi-Schulze
[
CCMS24
,
CCS23
]
and the resolution of the multiplicity one conjecture of mean curvature flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by Bamler and Kleiner
[
BK23
]
, it is known that starting from a generic closed embedded surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the mean curvature flow has only cylindrical or spherical singularities;

(2)

a mean convex mean curvature flow has only cylindrical or spherical singularities; c.f. the work of White
[
Whi00
,
Whi03
,
Whi15
]
, and other approaches by Sheng-Wang
[
SW09
]
, Andrews
[
And12
]
and Haslhofer-Kleiner
[
HK17
]
. In addition, Huisken-Sinestrari
[
HS99
]
proved that the
m
-convex
1
1

1

m
-convex means the sum of the smallest
m
number of principal curvatures at every point is nonnegative; mean convex is equivalent to
n
-convex of hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
mean curvature flow has only
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
as the tangent flow when
k\leq m-1
;

(3)

a mean curvature flow in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
starting from a closed embedded surface
\Sigma
has tangent flow at each point in the top stratum of the singular set to be a round cylinder; and if
\Sigma
has genus
0
, then the flow can have only cylindrical or spherical singularities, based on the work of Brendle
[
Bre16
]
and the recent resolution of the multiplicity one conjecture
[
BK23
]
;

(4)

a rotational symmetric mean curvature flow has only cylindrical or spherical singularities; c.f.
[
AAG95
]
.

Let us discuss some interesting special cases of our main theorem. In many situations mentioned above, the singularities of the flow must be cylindrical, and we can apply our main theorem. When the ambient space is
\mathbb{R}^{3}
or the initial data of the mean curvature flow is
2
-convex, the lower strata consist of isolated points, and the statement of our main theorem can be simplified.

Corollary 1.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{M}
is a mean curvature flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
starting from a closed embedded hypersurface
\mathbf{M}(0)
. Then
\forall\,\alpha\in(0,1)
, any non-
C^{2,\alpha}
-curve can not be the singular set of
\mathbf{M}
if

(1)

n=2
and
\mathbf{M}(0)
is mean convex or has genus
0
;

(2)

n\geq 3
and
\mathbf{M}(0)
is
2
-convex.

It would be interesting to know if
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
is not only
contained
in a
k
-dimensional submanifold, but itself
is
a
k
-dimensional submanifold. When
n=2
and
k=1
, this is a part of a conjecture by White
[
Whi02
, Page 533]
. When
k\geq 2
, we expect that
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
could be a non-smooth variety with dimension strictly less than
k
, such as the union of two curves that intersect at a point; see Remark
6.4
. In semilinear PDE, singularities with such an asymptotic profile have been constructed by Merle-Zaag in
[
MZ22
]
(although their singular set is still isolated).

We conjecture the following:

Conjecture 1.3
.

Suppose
n\geq 3,k\geq 2
. There exists a mean curvature flow
\mathbf{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
so that
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
is not a
k
-dimensional submanifold but a nonsmooth subvariety of dimension
\leq(k-1)
.

A key ingredient in proving Theorem
1.1
is a general asymptotic profile of cylindrical singularity. In
[
SX25b
]
, the first two authors proved a normal form theorem for rescaled mean curvature flow at a cylindrical singularity in a ball of size
O(\sqrt{\tau})
; c.f.
[
Gan21
,
Gan22
]
for some special generalized cylinders, and
[
ADS19
,
CHH22
,
CHHW22
,
DZ22
]
for the study of ancient asymptotically cylindrical flows. Motivated by the study of rotationally symmetric mean curvature flow by Angenent-Velázquez
[
AV97
]
, it is expected that if the asymptotic profile has a dominant term that decays exponentially fast, then the graphical region should expand exponentially.

To state our theorem on asymptotic profile, we introduce the following notations. Given a round cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, we use
\theta=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\cdots,\theta_{n-k+1})
to denote the coordinates on the sphere factor, and use
y=(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{k})
to denote the coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Note that the outward unit normal vector of
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
at
(\theta,y)
is
\hat{\theta}:=\theta/|\theta|
. It’s usually convenient to work within the following cube centered at
X=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
instead of balls:

Q_{r}(X):=B^{n-k+1}_{r}(x)\times B^{k}_{r}(y)\,,

and we shall omit
X
if
X=0
.

We shall use the following convention for “graphical functions”: given a hypersurface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
\delta\in(0,1]
and
r>\sqrt{2(n-k)}
, we call
\Sigma
a
\delta
-graph
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
in
Q_{r}
if there exists a function
v\in C^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\cap Q_{r})
with
\|v\|_{C^{2}}\leq\delta
such that

\Sigma\cap Q_{r}=\mathrm{graph}_{\mathcal{C}_{n,k}}(v):=\left\{\left(\theta+v(\theta,y)\hat{\theta},y\right):(\theta,y)\in\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\cap Q_{r}\right\}\,.

The maximal possible
r
that makes
\Sigma
a
\delta
-graph is called the
\delta
-graphical radius
of
\Sigma
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, denoted by
\mathbf{r}_{\delta}(\Sigma)
; and the corresponding
v\in C^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\cap Q_{r})
(extended by
0
to be defined on the whole
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
) is called the
graphical function
of
\Sigma
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
. (If there’s no such
r
that makes
\Sigma
a
\delta
-graph, we shall use the convention that
\mathbf{r}_{\delta}(\Sigma)=0
and the graphical function is also
0
.)
Similar notions can be generalized to Radon measures
\Sigma
.

The linearization of the graphical rescaled mean curvature flow equation over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
is given by
\partial_{\tau}u=L_{n,k}u
, where

L_{n,k}u=\Delta_{\mathcal{C}_{n,k}}u-\frac{1}{2}\langle X,\nabla_{\mathcal{C}_{n,k}}u\rangle+u\,.

We denote by
\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
the collection of Gaussian
L^{2}
-eigenvalues of
-L_{n,k}
, and for each
\gamma\in\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
, denote by
\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
the space of Gaussian
L^{2}
-eigenfunctions of
-L_{n,k}
with eigenvalue
\gamma
. A more precise discussion of them is included in Section
2.3
.

Using the technique in this paper, we proved the following trichotomy.

Theorem 1.4

(Asymptotic profile)
.

Let
\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
be a RMCF in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
over
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
which
C_{\loc}^{\infty}
-converges to
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
as
\tau\to\infty
. Then one of the following holds for the graphical function
v(\cdot,\tau)
and
1
-graphical radius
\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))
of
\mathcal{M}(\tau)
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
:

(i)

there exists
\mathcal{I}\subset\{1,2,\cdots,k\},\ \mathcal{I}\not=\emptyset
, and some constant
K=K(n,k)>0
such that after possibly a rotation in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
factor, for any
\tau
sufficiently large, we have
\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))\geq K\sqrt{\tau}
, and

\left\|v(\cdot,\tau)-\frac{\sqrt{2(n-k)}}{4\tau}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(y_{i}^{2}-2)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})}=o(\tau^{-1});

(ii)

there exist:

•

an eigenvalue
\gamma\in\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})\cap[1/2,\infty)
of
-L_{n,k}
,

•

an eigenfunction
\psi\in\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
of
L_{n,k}
with eigenvalue
\gamma
,

•

a rescaled mean curvature flow called “low spherical flow”, whose graphical function over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
is denoted by
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\in C^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
;

•

an explicit constant
\gamma^{+}:=\gamma^{+}(n,k,\gamma)>\gamma
(defined in
(II)
in Section
4
),

such that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\gamma)
and sufficiently large
\tau
, we have
\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))\geq e^{\frac{\gamma-\varepsilon}{2(\gamma+1)}\cdot\tau}
,

and

\left\|v(\cdot,\tau)-(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)+e^{-\gamma\tau}\psi)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})}=O(e^{-(\gamma^{+}-\varepsilon)\tau})\,;

(iii)

there exists a low spherical flow, whose graphical function is
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}
, such that for any
\lambda>0
and sufficiently large
\tau
, we have
\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))\geq e^{\frac{\lambda}{2(\lambda+1)}\cdot\tau}
and,

\|v(\cdot,\tau)-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})}=o(e^{-\lambda\tau})\,.

In the statement of this theorem, the “low spherical flows” are rescaled mean curvature flows near
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, whose graphical functions
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
satisfy

\begin{cases}\|\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{4}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{\tau}{n-k}},&\text{ when $n-k\geq 2$;}\\
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)=0,&\text{ when $n-k=1$}.\end{cases}

They are called “spherical flows” because they are invariant in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
directions, and hence only the spherical part matters; the word “low” indicates that when
n-k\geq 2
, they are the rescaled mean curvature flow converging to the sphere factor with the slowest rate. We construct a family of low spherical flows in Lemma
3.2
based on Appendix
A
. We include the term
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}
in our asymptotic profile because it represents the lowest order asymptotics when
n-k\geq 2
, but it does not contribute to the geometry of the singular set. We also remark that when
n-k=1
, there’s no spherical flow (except
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
itself) that represents the lowest order asymptotics on the cylinder, and we do not need them in our analysis, so we set them to be zero.

Remark 1.5
.

The case
(i)
of Theorem
1.4
has been proved in
[
SX25b
]
. Using our stratification of cylindrical singular set (
1.1
), a singularity
p\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
if and only if its RMCF
\mathcal{M}^{p}
satisfies case
(ii)
or
(iii)
after some rotation in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

In
[
AV97
]
, Angenent-Velázquez constructed examples of rotationally symmetric mean curvature flows that have the asymptotic profile as in
(ii)
near their singularities, and the graphical radii are proportional to
e^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}\tau}
. Therefore, our graphical radius bound
e^{\frac{\gamma-\varepsilon}{2(\gamma+1)}\tau}
obtained in
(ii)
is almost optimal. We believe that in case
(ii)
, the
\varepsilon
in the graphical radius bound can be removed. Nevertheless, as it is not necessary in this paper, we do not pursue this sharp graphical radius estimate.

The case
(iii)
is the case of super-exponential decay rate, and by the spirit of unique continuation of PDEs, it seems plausible that it only happens if
(v-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}})(\cdot,\tau)\equiv 0
.

Conjecture 1.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}
is a rescaled mean curvature flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
without boundary, and suppose the case
(iii)
of Theorem
1.4
holds. Then
\mathcal{M}
coincides with some low spherical flow.

Remark 1.7
.

A particularly interesting situation in
(ii)
is that
\gamma=1/2
(and for simplicity
n-k\neq 2
). In this situation, the eigenspace
\mathrm{W}_{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
is spanned by
\theta_{i}(y_{j}^{2}-2)
,
\theta_{i}y_{j_{1}}y_{j_{2}}
, and
y_{j}^{3}-6y_{j}
. If we collect all the terms of the first two types, we can write any such eigenfunction
\psi\in\mathrm{W}_{1/2}
as

\psi(\theta,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}|\theta|^{-1}\theta_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathbf{y}-2\tr\mathcal{A}_{i}),

where
\mathcal{A}_{i}
’s are symmetric
k\times k
matrices. These coefficients indeed record the geometric information of the singular set: if near a
k
-cylindrical singularity
p
, the singular set
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})
is a
k
-dimensional submanifold, then by Theorem
1.1

(i)
and
(ii)
, in a small neighborhood
U_{p}
of
p
, we have
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})\cap U_{p}=\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}\cap U_{p}
. After an appropriate rotation, let us assume the rescaled mean curvature flow
\mathcal{M}^{p}
converges to
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, then the tangent space
T_{p}(\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+})
is
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and our proof of Theorem
1.1
also yields:

•

if case
(ii)
happens with
\gamma=1/2
, then
2\mathcal{A}_{i}
is the projection of the second fundamental form of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
at
p
to the normal direction
\partial_{i}
;

•

if case
(ii)
happens with
\gamma>1/2
or case
(iii)
happens, then the second fundamental form of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
at
p
vanishes.

See the general statement in Proposition
6.6
.

In contrast, the terms
(y_{j}^{3}-6y_{j})
could be obstructions to the
k
-dimensionality and the manifold structure of the singular set. The mechanism behind this is similar to the singularities in
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}
. We refer the readers to Remark
6.4
.

1.1.
Regularity of singular sets of mean curvature flows

As a classical problem, the fine structure of singular sets has been studied in many other geometric and PDE problems, such as (far from a complete list) the minimal surfaces
[
Fed70
,
Alm83
,
Sim93
,
Whi97
]
,
semilinear PDEs
[
Zaa02a
,
Zaa02b
,
Zaa06
]
, and obstacle problem
[
FS19
,
FZ25
]
.

Let us review some history on the study of the cylindrical singular set of mean curvature flow. Given a self-shrinker
\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
\Sigma
being non-minimal, the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
factor is usually called the spine
3
3

3

In
[
Whi97
]
, the spine is defined for a general shrinking mean curvature flow, including the static or quasi-static cases.
of this shrinker. In
[
Whi97
]
, White proved that the singular set of mean curvature flow has a stratification based on the dimension of the spine. Furthermore, he proved that the singular set that is modeled by shrinkers with a
k
-dimensional spine has parabolic Hausdorff dimension at most
k
. Later, Cheeger-Haslhofer-Naber
[
CHN13
]
refined White’s result and proved a quantitative stratification theorem; for mean convex flow, this estimate was further refined recently by Hanbing Fang and Yu Li
[
FL25
]
.

The regularity of the cylindrical singular set was first studied by Colding-Minicozzi in their seminal work
[
CM16b
]
. They proved that the
k
-th strata
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})
are contained in finitely many
k
-dimensional Lipschitz submanifolds. Their proof shows that these submanifolds can be taken to be
C^{1}
, see the footnote of
[
CM18
, Page 815]
. The proof used their earlier result on the uniqueness of cylindrical tangent flow
[
CM15
]
, which allows one to compare the spines at two nearby cylindrical singularities.

It would be interesting to compare our result with the pioneering work in semilinear PDE by Zaag
[
Zaa02a
,
Zaa02b
,
Zaa06
]
. Zaag proved the
C^{2}
-regularity of the singular set of semilinear PDEs under the assumption that the singular set is a submanifold. The mechanism is similar to getting higher regularity of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}
for mean curvature flows, which we do not address in the current paper. The curvature of this singular set is due to the rotation of the degenerate direction within the spine. In contrast, Remark
1.7
suggests that the curvature of the degenerate cylindrical singular set originates from the spherical part, which is different from the semilinear PDE setting. Also, the semilinear PDE has only singularities for the first singular time. In contrast, the weak flow theory of MCF allows us to study all time singularities, even those appearing after the first singular time.

1.2.
Sketch of the proof

Our strategy of deriving higher regularity of singular sets is inspired by
[
FS19
]
. Intuitively, the singularities in
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
can only show up near the spine directions of each other. To show that
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
is locally contained in some
C^{\ell,\alpha}
submanifold
\Gamma
, we need to associate to each such singularity some “Whitney data” that will approximate
\Gamma
up to order
\ell
, estimate how close these assigned data are at different singularities, and then apply Whitney extension theorem to construct such
\Gamma
. When
\ell=2
, the Whitney data associated to
p\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
are:

•

a
k
-dimensional linear subspace
|\mathbf{L}_{p}|
, which will be the tangent direction of
\Gamma
at
p
, given by the spine of tangent flow of
\mathbf{M}
at
p
;

•

an
|\mathbf{L}_{p}|^{\perp}
-valued quadratic form
\mathbf{q}_{p}
on
|\mathbf{L}_{p}|
, which will be the second fundamental form of
\Gamma
at
p
, determined by the asymptotic profile of the RMCF
\mathcal{M}^{p}
as in Remark
1.7
. (For later reference, also let
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
be the pre-composition of
\mathbf{q}_{p}
with the orthogonal projection onto
|\mathbf{L}_{p}|
, which is an
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
-valued quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.)

Moreover, the “closeness” condition of Whitney data at two different singularities will (by a translation and rotation) pin down to the following location estimate: if

•

(\mathbf{0},0)\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
has Whitney data
|\mathbf{L}_{(\mathbf{0},0)}|=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\mathbf{q}_{(\mathbf{0},0)}=\mathbf{q}_{\circ}
(an
\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
-valued quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
) and
\mathbf{Q}_{(\mathbf{0},0)}=\mathbf{Q}_{\circ}
;

•

\bar{p}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
has Whitney data
\bar{\mathbf{L}},\bar{\mathbf{q}},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}
, and set
\bar{p}=(\bar{x},\bar{y},\bar{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}
, suppose
\max\{|\bar{x}|^{2}+|\bar{y}|^{2},|\bar{t}|\}=:r^{2}\in(0,1]
.

Then there exists a linear map
\mathbf{\ell}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
(also viewed as an
(n-k+1)\times k
-matrix) such that the rotation
e^{\bar{\mathbf{A}}}
maps the linear subspace
|\mathbf{L}_{\circ}|
to
|\bar{\mathbf{L}}|
, where
\bar{\mathbf{A}}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{\ell}\\
-\mathbf{\ell}^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}
; and the following estimate holds for every
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
:

(1.2)

\displaystyle r^{-2}|\bar{t}|+r^{-1}|\bar{x}-\mathbf{q}_{\circ}(\bar{y})|+\|\bar{\mathbf{\ell}}-\nabla\mathbf{q}_{\circ}(\bar{y})\|+r\|\bar{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{Q}_{\circ}\|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}r^{2\gamma^{+}_{n,k}-2\varepsilon}\,,

where
\gamma^{+}_{n,k}=\gamma^{+}(n,k,1/2)>1/2
is determined in the last bullet point of
(ii)
in Theorem
1.4
.

1.2.1.
Proof of location estimate

Taking Theorem
1.4
for granted, we now describe the proof idea of the location estimate. Recall that
(\mathbf{0},0)
being a degenerate cylindrical singularity implies, by Theorem
1.4

(ii)
and
(iii)
, that the RMCF
\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
based at
(0,0)
has its graphical function
v(\cdot,\tau)
satisfying the following asymptotic estimate,

(1.3)

\displaystyle v(\cdot,\tau)=\varphi_{\mathbf{u}_{\circ}}(\cdot,\tau)+e^{-\tau/2}\psi_{\circ}+O(e^{-(\gamma^{+}_{n,k}-\varepsilon)\tau})\,,

where
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}_{\circ}}
is the graphical function over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
of a low spherical flow,
\psi_{\circ}\in\mathrm{W}_{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
is an eigenfunction with eigenvalue
1/2
.
Note that we allow the scenario when
\psi_{\circ}=0
, which corresponds to faster asymptotics
\gamma>1/2
in case
(ii)
or case
(iii)
of Theorem
1.4
.

On the other hand, for every
r,\bar{p}
specified as above, we can write the RMCF
\mathcal{M}^{\bar{p}}
based at
\bar{p}
as the translation, dilation and time-reparametrization of
\mathcal{M}
:

\mathcal{M}^{\bar{p}}(\tau)=\sqrt{1-\bar{t}e^{\tau}}\cdot\mathcal{M}(\tau-\log(1-\bar{t}e^{\tau}))-e^{\tau/2}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\,.

By a quantitative uniqueness of tangent flow by Colding and Minicozzi
[
CM25
]
, when
\bar{p}
is close enough to
(0,0)
, we shall have

•

|\bar{t}|,|\bar{x}|^{2}\ll|\bar{y}|^{2}\sim r^{2}
;

•

|\bar{\mathbf{L}}|
is close to
|\mathbf{L}_{\circ}|
.

The second bullet point guarantees that there exists such a linear map
\mathbf{\ell}
as described above (
1.2
). In particular, the rotation
e^{-\bar{\mathbf{A}}}
maps
\mathcal{M}^{\bar{p}}
to a RMCF
\tau\mapsto\bar{\mathcal{M}}(\tau):=(e^{-\bar{\mathbf{A}}})_{\sharp}\mathcal{M}^{\bar{p}}(\tau)
which locally smoothly converges to
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
as
\tau\to+\infty
.
We proved in Appendix
C
that the graphical function
\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau)
of
\bar{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
is related to
v
by unwrapping these translation, dilation and rotation:

\bar{v}(\theta,y;\tau)\sim v(\theta,y+\mathbf{y},\tau^{\prime})+\varrho(\bar{\lambda}-1)-\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{x}}}-\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{A}}}\,,

where
\varrho:=\sqrt{2(n-k)}
and

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}:=\tau-\log(1-\bar{t}e^{\tau})\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}:=\sqrt{1-\bar{t}e^{\tau}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{x}}:=e^{\tau/2}\bar{x}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{y}}:=e^{\tau/2}\bar{y}\,;

and
\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{x}}},\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{A}}}
are eigenfunctions of
-L_{n,k}
induced by translation and rotation (see Section
2.3.3
for their precise definitions). Combined with (
1.3
), this implies that if we choose
\tau
so that
|\bar{\mathbf{y}}|\sim 1
(which means
r\sim|\bar{y}|\sim e^{-\tau/2}
), then

(1.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{v}(\theta,y;\tau)\sim\varphi_{\mathbf{u}_{\circ}}(\theta;\tau^{\prime})+e^{-\tau^{\prime}/2}\psi_{\circ}(\theta,y+\mathbf{y})+\varrho(\bar{\lambda}-1)-\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{x}}}-\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{A}}}+O(e^{-(\gamma^{+}_{n,k}-\varepsilon)\tau})\,.\end{split}

On the other hand, since
\bar{p}
is also a degenerate cylindrical singularity for
\mathbf{M}
, Theorem
1.4

(ii)
,
(iii)
also applies to
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
, which gives the asymptotic estimate,

\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau)=\varphi_{\bar{\mathbf{u}}}(\cdot,\tau)+e^{-\tau/2}\bar{\psi}+O(e^{-(\gamma^{+}_{n,k}-\varepsilon)\tau})

for some low spherical flow
\varphi_{\bar{\mathbf{u}}}
and eigenfunction
\bar{\psi}\in\mathrm{W}_{1/2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
. Combined with (
1.4
), this implies (modulo estimates on the errors),

\displaystyle\left\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}_{\circ}}-\varphi_{\bar{\mathbf{u}}})(\cdot,\tau)+e^{-\tau^{\prime}/2}\left(\psi_{\circ}(\cdot+\mathbf{y})-\bar{\psi}\right)+\left(\varrho(\bar{\lambda}-1)-\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{x}}}-\psi_{\bar{\mathbf{A}}}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim e^{-(\gamma^{+}_{n,k}-\varepsilon)\tau}\,.

Projecting the left-hand side onto the eigenspaces of
-L_{n,k}
with eigenvalues
-1,-1/2,0,1/(n-k),1/2
proves the estimates in each term of the location estimate (
1.2
).

Let us finish this discussion by comparing our approach with that employed in the obstacle problem by Figalli-Serra
[
FS19
]
and Franceschini-Zatoń
[
FZ25
]
.
In these works, the object of study is a function
u
solving a certain PDE. At each singularity belonging to the subset with improved regularity, one can approximate
u
by polynomials to arbitrarily high order; singular points that do not admit such increasingly accurate polynomial approximations are shown to form a lower-dimensional set.
In contrast, in the setting of MCF
\mathbf{M}
, due to the nature of nonlinearity, it is hard to exhaust the ‘‘approximate solutions” at the level of the flow that would play the role of polynomials
4
4

4

To obtain
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity, our approximations are tangent flows, low spherical flows and eigenfunctions of Jacobi operator with eigenvalue
1/2
. For example, higher-order expansions at singularities may naturally contain spherical modes: these do not influence the geometry of the singular set (and thus the singularity need not belong to a lower stratum), yet they still contribute to the approximation of the flow.
Moreover, translating such approximations of
\mathbf{M}
at the level of flow into Whitney data that capture the singular set and verifying the compatibility condition is more involved than in the polynomial case.
For these reasons, we only get
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity for singular sets in the MCF setting instead of
C^{\infty}
.

1.2.2.
Some ingredients in proving Theorem
1.4

Let us briefly describe the proof of the asymptotic profile Theorem
1.4
in the case
n-k=1
(where we don’t need to modulo low spherical flows) and the flow decays exponentially toward the cylinder. For a RMCF
\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
with entropy
\leq\varepsilon^{-1}
, in
[
SWX25
]
, we introduced the “(Gaussian)
L^{2}
-distance”
\mathbf{d}_{n,k}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))
to the cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
and the “decay order” (a discrete parabolic analog of Almgren’s frequency function):

\mathcal{N}_{n,k}(\tau,\mathcal{M}):=\ln\left(\frac{\mathbf{d}_{n,k}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))}{\mathbf{d}_{n,k}(\mathcal{M}(\tau+1))}\right)\,,

which measures how fast
\mathbf{d}_{n,k}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))
is decaying near
\tau
-time slice. In
[
SWX25
, Section 3]
, we proved a new
L^{2}
-nonconcentration property of rescaled mean curvature flow and, as an application, a discrete monotonicity of decay order, which roughly speaking has the following useful consequences:

•

if
\mathcal{M}(\tau)
converges locally smoothly to
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
as
\tau\to+\infty
, then
\lim\limits_{\tau\to+\infty}\mathcal{N}_{n,k}(\tau,\mathcal{M})
is either
\infty
or an eigenvalue of
L_{n,k}
;

•

for every
\tau^{\prime}\geq 0
, we have the following universal bound (recall that
-1
is the smallest eigenvalue of
-L_{n,k}
)

\mathbf{d}_{n,k}(\mathcal{M}(\tau+\tau^{\prime}))\lesssim_{n,\varepsilon}e^{(1+\varepsilon)\tau^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{n,k}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))\,.

With these two bullet points and a novel translation argument, we can prove the graphical radius of a RMCF
\mathcal{M}(\tau)
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
is
\gtrsim e^{\frac{\gamma-\varepsilon}{2(\gamma+1)}\cdot\tau}
when
\lim\limits_{\tau\to+\infty}\mathcal{N}_{n,k}(\tau,\mathcal{M})=\gamma<+\infty
, see a precise discussion in Lemma
4.3
. After estimating the graphical radius, we can write
\mathcal{M}(\tau)
as a graph of some
v(\cdot,\tau)\in C^{2}
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
in an exponentially expanding cube. The RMCF equation on
\mathcal{M}
can be interpreted as a parabolic equation of
v
,

(\partial_{\tau}-L_{n,k})v=\mathcal{Q}(u,\nabla u,\nabla^{2}u)\,,

where the right-hand side is a quadratic error term. The argument above also gives an a priori spacetime
C^{2}
estimate on
v
of form

\|v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\cap Q_{R})}\lesssim_{n,\varepsilon}e^{-(\gamma-\varepsilon)\tau}R^{2(1+\gamma)}\,,

which leads to an upper bound on the error term decaying like
e^{-2(\gamma-\varepsilon)\tau}
, faster than
e^{-\gamma\tau}
. Making use of this, the principal decaying mode can be extracted from
v(\cdot,\tau)
with an error estimate by a purely PDE argument.

As a final remark about the proof of Theorem
1.4
, when
n-k\geq 2
, one needs to replace the
L^{2}
-distance and decay order above by the ones relative to a low spherical flow to reprove the discrete monotonicity and its consequences. This is done in Section
3
.

1.2.3.
Structure of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}

Finally we discuss the structure of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}
. These singularities have asymptotic profiles as indicated in item
(i)
of Theorem
1.4
. When
\mathcal{I}=\{1,2,\cdots,k\}
, the singularities are called nondegenerate, and have been studied extensively in
[
SWX25
]
. In particular, they are isolated in spacetime. When
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{I}\subsetneqq\{1,2,\cdots,k\}
, the singularities are called
partially nondegenerate
, and they carry the structure of nondegenerate singularities in the directions generated by
\{y_{i}|i\in\mathcal{I}\}
in the spine. In particular, the asymptotic profile allows us to conclude that the nearby singularities can not approach a partially nondegenerate singularity from those nondegenerate directions. Equivalently, this shows that the partially nondegenerate singularities can only stay close to the degenerate directions in the spine. Hence, the Hausdorff dimension of partially nondegenerate singularities must be smaller.

It is plausible to get higher regularity of
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}
, for example, by combining the ideas in the current paper with the idea of Zaag in
[
Zaa02a
,
Zaa02b
,
Zaa06
]
. Nevertheless, the technical details can be very different from the current paper.

1.3.
Organization of the paper

After a preliminary section, Section
2
, we define and discuss the family of low spherical flows, and basic properties of the relative decay order
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{u}}
in Section
3
. In Section
4
, we discuss the key asymptotic profile analysis at the singularities in
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
and the proof of Theorem
1.4
. In Section
5
, we discuss how to use the results in Section
4
to estimate the location of singularities in
\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{+}
. In Section
6
, we prove Theorem
1.1
by using the Whitney extension theorem, based on the location estimates derived in Section
5
. Finally, we construct in Appendix
A
a continuous family of convex mean curvature flows with prescribed asymptotics to the round sphere, and prove some key properties of this family. We collect basic properties on parabolic Jacobi fields and geometry of graph over the round cylinder in Appendices
B
and
C
. These will be used throughout the paper.

1.4.
Acknowledgment

We thank Sigurd Angenent, Toby Colding, Alessio Figalli, and Bill Minicozzi for their interest.

2.
Preliminary

2.1.
Parabolic metric spacetime and Hausdorff measure

We recall the following geometric structure of the parabolic spacetime. Throughout this paper, the ambient space is
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and the ambient spacetime is
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
.
For
X_{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\lambda>0
, we let
\eta_{X_{\circ},\lambda}:X\mapsto X_{\circ}+\lambda X
denote the translation and dilation map. For a subset
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
or a varifold
V
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we may simply denote their translation and dilation by
\lambda S+X_{\circ}:=\eta_{X_{\circ},\lambda}(S)
,
\lambda V+X_{\circ}:=(\eta_{X_{\circ},\lambda})_{\sharp}V
.

For
(X_{1},t_{1}),\,(X_{2},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
, the parabolic distance between them is defined to be

d_{\mathcal{P}}((X_{1},t_{1}),(X_{2},t_{2}))=\max\{|X_{1}-X_{2}|,|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}\}.

In particular, the metric ball of radius
r
centered at the spacetime origin
(\mathbf{0},0)
is the parabolic neighborhood
B_{r}(\mathbf{0})\times[-r^{2},r^{2}]
, denoted by
P_{r}(\mathbf{0},0)
.

Recall that given
\beta>0
and
\delta\in(0,\infty]
, the parabolic Hausdorff premeasures
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{P},\delta}(E)
of a subset
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
are defined as follows (up to a dimensional constant multiple):

(2.1)

\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{P},\delta}(E):=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{i}{\rm diam}(E_{i})^{\beta}\,:\,E\subset\bigcup_{i}E_{i},\,{\rm diam}(E_{i})<\delta\biggr\}.

Here
{\rm diam}(E_{i})
is the diameter of
E_{i}
measured under
d_{\mathcal{P}}
. The parabolic Hausdorff measure is then defined as
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{P}}(E):=\lim\limits_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{P},\delta}(E)
, and the parabolic Hausdorff dimension is

\dim_{\mathcal{P}}(E):=\inf\{\beta\geq 0:\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{P}}(E)=0\}\,.

Note that if
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, then
\mathcal{H}^{k}(K\times\{0\})
is just the usual Hausdorff measure of
K
in the Euclidean space; on the other hand, the time axis
\{\mathbf{0}\}\times\mathbb{R}
has parabolic Hausdorff dimension
2
.

The following lemma is a variant of
[
FS19
, Lemma 3.5]
in the parabolic setting, whose proof also generalizes directly, and we skip it here. It will be used to estimate
\dim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{S}_{k}(\mathbf{M})_{0}
in Section
6.1
.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
FS19
, Lemma 3.5]
)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
be a set with
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{P}}(E)>0
for some
\beta\in(0,n+2]
. Then for
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{P}}
-almost every point
p_{\circ}\in E
, there is a sequence
r_{j}\downarrow 0
such that the following “accumulation set” for
E
at
0
:

\mathcal{A}_{E,p_{\circ}}:=\left\{z\in\overline{P_{1/2}}\,:\exists\,(j_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\nearrow+\infty,(z_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}\text{ s.t. $z_{\ell}\in r_{j_{\ell}}^{-1}.(E-p_{\circ})\cap P_{1/2}$ and $z_{\ell}\to z$}\right\},

(where
r_{j_{\ell}}^{-1}.E:=\{(r_{j_{\ell}}^{-1}X,r_{j_{\ell}}^{-2}t):(X,t)\in E\}
denotes the parabolic dilation of
E
) satisfies

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{P}}^{\beta}(\mathcal{A}_{E,p_{\circ}})>0\,.

2.2.
Brakke flow and rescaled mean curvature flow

We recall that a (classic) mean curvature flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
over an interval
I
is a family of smooth embeddings
F:M\times I\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
of an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
, satisfying the equation
\partial_{t}F(x,t)=\vec{H}(F(x,t))
.
In order to study singular sets, we need to work with weak flows.

Definition 2.2
.

An
n
-dimensional
(integral) Brakke flow
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
over an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a one-parameter family of Radon measures
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{t\in I}
, such that

(1)

for almost every
t\in I
,
\Sigma_{t}
is a Radon measure associated to an
n
-dimensional integral varifold with mean curvature
\vec{H}_{t}\in L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})
,

(2)

for every non-negative function
\Phi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times I)
, we have

(2.2)

\frac{d}{dt}\int\Phi\ d\Sigma_{t}\leq\int\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}+\nabla\Phi\cdot\vec{H}_{t}-\Phi|\vec{H}_{t}|^{2}\right)\ d\Sigma_{t},

in the distribution sense.

The support of the Brakke flow is defined to be
\spt(\Sigma):=\overline{\bigcup_{t}\spt(\Sigma_{t})\times\{t\}}
, where the closure is taken in the spacetime.

Definition 2.3
.

A point
p_{\circ}=(X_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
in the support of
\mathbf{M}
is called a
regular point
of
\mathbf{M}
if there exists
r>0
such that in
B_{r}(X_{\circ})
,
t\mapsto\mathbf{M}(t)
is the area measure associated to a classical mean curvature flow over
(t_{\circ}-r^{2},t_{\circ}]
; otherwise,
p_{\circ}
is called a
singularity
of
\mathbf{M}
.

We say that a sequence of flow
\{\Sigma_{t}^{j}\}_{t\in I}
converges to
\{\Sigma_{t}^{\infty}\}_{t\in I}

in the Brakke sense
, if
\Sigma^{j}_{t}
measure-converges to
\Sigma^{\infty}_{t}
for all
t\in I
, and the associated varifolds converge for a.e.
t\in I
.
We will use the important Brakke-White’s
\varepsilon
-regularity
[
Whi05
]
, which implies that if
\{\Sigma_{t}^{j}\}_{t\in I}
converges to
\{\Sigma_{t}^{\infty}\}_{t\in I}
in the Brakke sense and
\{\Sigma_{t}^{\infty}\}_{t\in I}
is a classic mean curvature flow of smooth hypersurfaces, then for every compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times I
and
j\gg 1
(depending on
K
), inside
K
,
\{\Sigma_{t}^{j}\}_{t\in I}
is also a classic mean curvature flow of smooth hypersurfaces, and the convergence is smooth.

We remark that another important weak flow is the so-called level set flow, which is closely related to the Brakke flow whenever it does not fatten. We refer the readers to the preliminary section of
[
SWX25
]
for a more comprehensive preliminary study of weak flows of mean curvature flow, and
[
Bra78
,
Ilm94
,
Whi95
]
for detailed discussions. In particular, the level set flow of convex mean curvature flow can be formulated as the
arrival time function
. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a convex open subset, then a twice differentiable function
\mathbf{u}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is called the arrival time function if it satisfies the following equation

(2.3)

\displaystyle|\nabla\mathbf{u}|^{2}(\Delta\mathbf{u}+1)=\nabla^{2}\mathbf{u}(\nabla\mathbf{u},\nabla\mathbf{u})\,.

We refer the readers to
[
ES91
,
Ilm92
,
Whi95
,
CM16a
]
, among other references, for further discussions of the arrival time functions.

Huisken
[
Hui90
]
introduced the “rescaled mean curvature flow” to study singularities. Given a Brakke flow
t\mapsto\mathbf{M}(t)
and a spacetime point
p_{\circ}=(X_{\circ},t_{\circ})
, one can define a new flow
\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}:\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}(\tau)
associated to
\mathbf{M}
, called the
rescaled mean curvature flow (RMCF)
of
\mathbf{M}
based at
p_{\circ}
, whose time slices are defined by

(2.4)

\mathcal{M}^{p_{\circ}}(\tau)=e^{\tau/2}(\mathbf{M}(t_{\circ}-e^{-\tau})-X_{\circ}).

Huisken observed that the rescaled mean curvature flow is a gradient flow of the Gaussian area, which is known as Huisken’s monotonicity formula. Recall that given a Radon measure
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, its
n
-dimensional
Gaussian area
is defined by

\mathcal{F}[\Sigma]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(4\pi)^{-n/2}e^{-\frac{|X|^{2}}{4}}d\Sigma(X).

In particular, for a hypersurface
M
, we denote for simplicity
\mathcal{F}[M]:=\mathcal{F}[\|M\|]
, where
\|M\|
is the
n
-dimensional volume measure associated to
M
. As a consequence of Huisken’s monotonicity formula, we define the
Gaussian density
of a mean curvature flow at the spacetime point
p
as

\Theta_{\mathbf{M}}(p):=\lim\limits_{\tau\to+\infty}\mathcal{F}[\mathcal{M}^{p}(\tau)]\,.

It is worth noting that the Gaussian density is upper semi-continuous in the following sense: for a sequence of MCFs
\mathbf{M}_{j}
converging to
\mathbf{M}_{\infty}
in Brakke sense, and a sequence of spacetime points
p_{j}
converging to
p_{\infty}
, we have
\Theta_{\mathbf{M}_{\infty}}(p_{\infty})\geq\limsup_{j}\Theta_{\mathbf{M}_{j}}(p_{j})
; see
[
IW15
, Page 4]
.
As a consequence, every
p\in\spt(\mathbf{M})
has Gaussian density
\Theta_{\mathbf{M}}(p)\geq 1
, and Brakke-White’s
\varepsilon
-regularity guarantees that equality holds if and only if
p
is a regular point of
\mathbf{M}
.
We say an integral Brakke flow is
unit regular
if every point with Gaussian density
1
has a space-time neighborhood where it is smooth. By White
[
Whi05
]
, being unit regular is preserved under Brakke convergence of MCF.

Colding-Minicozzi
[
CM12
]
introduced the
entropy
of a hypersurface (or an
n
-varifold):

\lambda[M]:=\sup_{(X_{\circ},t_{\circ})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\mathcal{F}[t_{\circ}^{-1}(M-X_{\circ})].

By Huisken’s monotonicity formula, entropy is monotone nonincreasing along a Brakke flow. For a Brakke flow
t\mapsto\mathbf{M}(t)
or a RMCF
\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
over
I
, we define their entropy as

\lambda[\mathbf{M}]:=\sup_{t\in I}\lambda[\mathbf{M}(t)]\,,\qquad\lambda[\mathcal{M}]:=\sup_{t\in I}\lambda[\mathcal{M}(t)]\,.

An important feature of the Gaussian area is that it decays very fast at infinity. In fact, we will frequently use the following proposition, especially when
\Sigma=\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
:

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a hypersurface (or an
n
-varifold) with
\lambda[\Sigma]<+\infty
. Then for any
N>0
and
R>0
,

\Psi_{\Sigma,N}(R):=\int_{\Sigma\backslash B_{R}}(1+|X|^{N})e^{-\frac{|X|^{2}}{4}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}(X)\lesssim_{n,N,\delta}\lambda[\Sigma]\cdot e^{-\frac{R^{2}}{4+\delta}}.

Proof.

First recall that by
[
CM12
, Lemma 2.9]
, for any
r>0
,

\area(\Sigma\cap B_{r}(\mathbf{0}))\lesssim_{n}\lambda[\Sigma]\cdot r^{n}

Therefore, for any
R>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma\backslash B_{R}}(1+|X|^{N})e^{-\frac{|X|^{2}}{4}}\ dX

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma\cap(B_{R+j}\backslash B_{R+j-1})}(1+|X|^{N})e^{-\frac{|X|^{2}}{4}}\ dX

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\lambda[\Sigma]\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(1+(R+j)^{N+n}\right)\cdot e^{-\frac{(R+j-1)^{2}}{4}}\ \ \lesssim_{n,N,\delta}\lambda[\Sigma]\cdot e^{-\frac{R^{2}}{4+\delta}}\,.

where the last inequality is a consequence of the fast decay of the exponential factor.
∎

Throughout this paper, unless otherwise noted, a “mean curvature flow (MCF)” is referred to as a unit regular Brakke flow with finite entropy, and a “rescaled mean curvature flow (RMCF)” means the RMCF of some mean curvature flow based at some point. Also for a Radon measure
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we may abuse the notation to use
\int_{\Sigma}\cdot
instead of
\int\cdot\ d\Sigma
to denote the integration with respect to
\Sigma
, and use
\Sigma\cap B
instead of
\Sigma\llcorner B
to denote the restriction of
\Sigma
onto
B
.

2.3.
Geometry of generalized cylinders

We use
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to denote the round generalized cylinder
S^{n-k}(\varrho)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}=:\mathbb{S}^{n-k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
\varrho=\varrho_{n,k}:=\sqrt{2(n-k)}
; and we use
(\theta,y)=(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n-k+1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{k})
as the cylindrical coordinates over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, where
\theta_{i}
is the restriction of the coordinate function
x_{i}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
to
\mathbb{S}^{n-k}
. And we use
\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}):=\{\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}):\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{O}(n+1)\}
to denote all possible rotations of
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
.

2.3.1.
The rotation group and its subgroups

The subgroup of
\mathrm{O}(n+1)
that preserves
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
is

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{G}_{n,k}:=\{\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{O}(n+1):\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})=\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\}=\mathrm{O}(n-k+1)\times\mathrm{O}(k)\,.

We let
\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}
be its Lie algebra, and
\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}_{n,k}
be the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}
with respect to the metric
\langle\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2}\rangle:=\tr(\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\top})
on
\mathfrak{o}(n+1)
. Note that,

•

\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}
consists of
(n+1)\times(n+1)
skew symmetric matrices of form:

\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{1}&0\\
0&\mathbf{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\,,

under the decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}=\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Therefore
\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}^{\perp}
consists of
(n+1)\times(n+1)
skew symmetric matrices of the following form under this decomposition:

\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{\ell}\\
-\mathbf{\ell}^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\,,

where
\mathbf{\ell}
is an arbitrary
(n-k+1)\times k
matrix, which can also be viewed as a linear map from
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}\times\{0\}
.

•

By implicit function theorem, there exists
\kappa_{n}\in(0,1)
such that

\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}\to\mathrm{O}(n+1)\,,\quad(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime})\mapsto e^{\mathbf{A}}e^{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}

is a diffeomorphism from the
\kappa_{n}
-ball centered at
\mathbf{0}
on the domain to an open neighborhood of the identity matrix in
\mathrm{O}(n+1)
. By possibly shrinking
\kappa_{n}
, we can also require

\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}^{\perp}\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})\,,\quad\mathbf{A}\mapsto e^{\mathbf{A}}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k})

to be a diffeomorphism from the
\kappa_{n}
-ball centered at
\mathbf{0}
on the domain to an open neighborhood of
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
in the
k
-Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
.
Note that when
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}
,
e^{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{G}_{n,k}
preserves
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
. Hence, to study the effect of rotations on the
L^{2}
-distance function to round cylinders, it suffices to work only in
\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}^{\perp}
.

2.3.2.
The
L
-operator and the weighted
L^{2}
-space

It is natural to define the weighted Gaussian
L^{2}
-norm: for a hypersurface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
, define

\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}:=(4\pi)^{-n/2}\int_{M}|f(X)|^{2}e^{-\frac{|X|^{2}}{4}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}(X).

Throughout this paper, without a specific notion, all the
L^{2}
-norms refer to this weighted one. When
M=\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, we sometimes replace the subscript
L^{2}(M)
simply by
L^{2}
to save notation.

We are interested in the following linear operator on
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, known as the
Jacobi operator
for shrinker:

(2.6)

L_{n,k}\,u=\Delta_{\mathcal{C}_{n,k}}u-\frac{1}{2}\langle y,\nabla_{y}u\rangle+u=\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-k}}u+(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}+1)u\,,

which is self-adjoint with respect to the
L^{2}
norm above, where

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}:=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}\partial_{y_{i}}

is the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

2.3.3.
The spectrum of the
L
-operator

Throughout this paper, we denote by
\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
the collection of
L^{2}
-eigenvalues of
-L_{n,k}
(i.e.
\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
if and only if there exists a nonzero function
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
satisfying
-L_{n,k}\,f=\lambda f
). For
\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
, we denote by
\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})\subset L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
the space of (
L^{2}
-)eigenfunctions of
-L_{n,k}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
. And for
\sim\in\{\geq,>,=,\neq,<,\leq\}
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by

(2.7)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\sim\gamma}:L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})

the (
L^{2}
-)orthogonal projection onto the direct sum
\oplus_{\lambda\sim\gamma}\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}
. A particularly important eigenvalue is
\gamma_{n,k}:=\frac{1}{n-k}
, which we will use in multiple places.

In
[
SWZ24
, Section 5.2]
, it was proved that the eigenvalues of
-L_{n,k}
are given by

(2.8)

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})=\{\mu_{i}+j/2-1\}_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\,,

with corresponding eigenspaces given by

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})=\operatorname{span}\big\{\phi_{i}(\theta)h_{j}(y):\mu_{i}+j/2-1=\lambda\big\}\,,

where
\mu_{i}=\frac{i(i-1+n-k)}{2(n-k)}
and
\phi_{i}
are eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-k}}
(
\phi_{i}
are known to be the restriction of homogeneous harmonic polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
), and
h_{j}
are Hermitian polynomials of degree
j
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, which are eigenfunctions of
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}
with eigenvalue
j/2
. Note that,

•

degree
0
Hermitian polynomials are constants; degree
1
Hermitian polynomials are linear functions on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
;

•

any degree
2
Hermitian polynomials can be written as

h_{2}(y)=q(y)-2\tr q\,,

where
q
is a homogeneous quadratic polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and (letting
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
)
\tr q:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}q(e_{j})\in\mathbb{R}
.

For later reference, for
\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{O}(n+1)
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
we also let

\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}):=\{\psi\circ\mathbf{B}^{-1}:\psi\in\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})\}\,.

Note that this is well-defined since
\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
is
\mathrm{G}_{n,k}
-invariant.

We remark that when
(n-k)\geq 2
, the smallest positive eigenvalue in
\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
is
1/(n-k)\leq 1/2
. Therefore, the spectrum behaviors of
\mathcal{C}_{n,n-1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
with
k\leq n-2
are slightly different.
In the following, we list the first few eigenspaces of
-L_{n,k}
and their geometric meanings:

(i)

\mathrm{W}_{-1}=\{c:c\in\mathbb{R}\}
consists of constant function;

(ii)

\mathrm{W}_{-1/2}
consists of linear combinations of linear functions
h_{1}(y)
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
translation-like functions

\psi_{\mathbf{x}}(\theta)
on
\mathbb{S}^{n-k}
, where for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
, we define (set
\hat{\theta}:=\theta/|\theta|
)

(2.10)

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{x}}(\theta):=\mathbf{x}\cdot\hat{\theta}\,;

For later reference, we denote by
\mathrm{W}_{\Tr}
the space of translation-like functions on
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
.

(iii)

\mathrm{W}_{0}
consists of linear combinations of quadratic Hermitian polynomials
h_{2}(y)
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
rotation-like functions

\psi_{\mathbf{A}}(\theta,y)
on
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, where for
\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{\ell}\\
-\mathbf{\ell}^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathfrak{g}_{n,k}^{\perp}
we define,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{A}}(\theta,y):=\langle\mathbf{A}(0,y),(\hat{\theta},0)\rangle=(\mathbf{\ell}\,y)\cdot\hat{\theta}\,.

Again for later reference, we denote by
\mathrm{W}_{\operatorname{Rot}}
the space of rotation-like functions on
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
.

(iv)

When
n-k\neq 2
,
\mathrm{W}_{1/2}
consists of linear combinations of cubic Hermitian polynomials
h_{3}(y)
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and linear combinations of products of translation-like functions and quadratic Hermitian polynomials, which takes the form

\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}\psi_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}(\theta)(q_{i}(y)-2\tr q_{i})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}\big(q_{i}(y)-2\tr q_{i}\big)\mathbf{e}_{i}\right)\cdot\hat{\theta}=(\mathbf{q}(y)-2\tr\mathbf{q})\cdot\hat{\theta}\,;

where
\{\mathbf{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n-k+1}
is the coordinate orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
,
q_{i}
are homogeneous quadratic polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
(possibly
0
), and

\mathbf{q}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}q_{i}\,\mathbf{e}_{i}:\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}\times\{0\}

is an
\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
-valued homogeneous quadratic polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Such functions play an essential role in the
C^{2}
-regularity of the
k
-cylindrical singular set. When
n-k=2
,
\mathrm{W}_{1/2}
is the span of these functions discussed above together with the following
low spherical modes
:

(v)

Let
\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}\subset\mathrm{W}_{1/(n-k)}
be the space of 2nd eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-k}}
(i.e. those with eigenvalue
1/(n-k)+1
), extended
y
-invariantly as functions on
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
. By the classification of harmonic polynomial,
\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}
consists of functions of form

\psi(\theta,y)=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{n-k+1}a_{i_{1}i_{2}}\theta_{i_{1}}\theta_{i_{2}}\,,

where
\sum a_{ii}=0
. These functions describe the slowest asymptotic in the spherical factor. In fact, each mode corresponds to some rescaled mean curvature flow converging to the sphere with the corresponding leading order asymptotics, see
[
Ses08
,
Str20
]
, as well as Appendix
A
in this paper. These modes do not affect the
C^{2}
regularity of the singularity set, but we need to modulo these modes because they may have lower eigenvalues.

In summary, we have the following list of the first several eigenfunctions of
-L_{n,k}
.

eigenvalues of
-L_{n,k}

corresponding eigenfunctions (not necessarily normalized)

-1

1

-1/2

\theta_{i},y_{j}

0

\theta_{i}y_{j},\ y_{j}^{2}-2,\ y_{j_{1}}y_{j_{2}}

1/2

\theta_{i}(y_{j}^{2}-2)
,

\theta_{i}(y_{j_{1}}y_{j_{2}})
,

y_{j}^{3}-6y_{j}
,
\dots

1/(n-k)

\theta_{i_{1}}^{2}-\theta_{i_{2}}^{2}
,
\dots

Table 1
.
Eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of
L_{n,k}
.

2.3.4.
Two facts on eigenfunctions

The following lemma will be used in Section
5
in getting bounds on Whitney data.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
h
be a Hermitian polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
of degree
l\geq 2
. Then for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
and every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
|\mathbf{y}|\geq 1
, we have

\|a+h(\cdot+\mathbf{y})-h\|_{L^{2}}\geq c(k,l)|\mathbf{y}|^{1-l}|a|\,.

Proof.

Because Hermitian polynomials after a rotation are still Hermitian polynomials, without loss of generality, we may assume
\mathbf{y}=(L,0,0,\cdots,0)
. Recall that a Hermitian polynomial
h
with degree
l
satisfies the equation

\Delta h-\frac{1}{2}y\cdot\nabla h+\frac{l}{2}h=0.

This implies that

\displaystyle I:=\int(h(y+\mathbf{y})-h(y))d\mu=

\displaystyle\int\big(\frac{2}{l}\mathcal{L}(h(y+\mathbf{y})-h(y))-\frac{1}{l}\mathbf{y}\cdot\nabla h(y+\mathbf{y})\big)d\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\frac{1}{l}\mathbf{y}\cdot\nabla h(y+\mathbf{y})d\mu=-\int\frac{1}{l}L\partial_{1}h(y+\mathbf{y})d\mu.

Note that the derivative of an Hermitian polynomial is still an Hermitian polynomial with eigenvalue shifted by
1/2
. Iterate the above argument
l
-times, we get

I=\left(\int 1d\mu\right)(-1)^{l}L^{l}c_{l,0,\cdots,0}\,,

where
c_{l,0,\cdots,0}
is the coefficient of the monomial
y_{1}^{l}
in the polynomial
h
.

By projection to the constant, we have

\|a+h(\cdot+\mathbf{y})-h(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\geq\left|a\left(\int 1d\mu\right)+I\right|.

If
\left(\int 1d\mu\right)^{-1}|I|<a/2
, then
\|a+h(\cdot+\mathbf{y})-h(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\geq|a|/2\geq|\mathbf{y}|^{1-l}|a|/2
.

If
\left(\int 1d\mu\right)^{-1}|I|\geq a/2
, then at least one of
|L^{l}c_{l,0,\cdots,0}|\geq C|a|
. Let
p
be the unique Hermitian polynomial with degree
l-1
and leading order monomial
y_{1}^{l-1}
. Then

\displaystyle\int(a+h(y+\mathbf{y})-h(y))p(y)d\mu=

\displaystyle\int(h(y+\mathbf{y})-h(y))p(y)d\mu.

Note that
h(y+\mathbf{y})-h(y)
has degree
l-1
, and it has the monomial term
y_{1}^{l-1}
with coefficient
lLc_{l,0,\cdots,0}
, so the projection of
h(y+\mathbf{y})-h(y)
to
p(y)
has length greater than or equal to
C|lLc_{l,0,\cdots,0}|
. This shows that

\|a+h(\cdot+\mathbf{y})-h(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\geq C|lLc_{l,0,\cdots,0}|\geq CL^{1-l}|a|=C|\mathbf{y}|^{1-l}|a|.

In the above discussion, the constant
C
varies line by line but is uniformly bounded by
l
and
k
.
∎

The following lemma estimates the difference of the
L^{2}
norm after a cut-off.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\zeta\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
be such that
0\leq\zeta\leq 1
and
\zeta|_{Q_{R}}=1
;
\Pi:L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
be the orthogonal projection map onto a closed linear subspace. Then for every
v\in L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
, we have

\big|\|\Pi(v\zeta)\|_{L^{2}}-\|\Pi(v)\|_{L^{2}}\big|\leq\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\setminus Q_{R})}

In particular, if
\Lambda>0
,
v\in\oplus_{\gamma\leq\Lambda}\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}
, then

\big|\|\Pi(v\zeta)\|_{L^{2}}-\|\Pi(v)\|_{L^{2}}\big|\lesssim_{n,\Lambda}e^{-R^{2}/10}\|v\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality,

\|\Pi(v\zeta)\|_{L^{2}}-\|\Pi(v)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\Pi(v(1-\zeta))\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|v(1-\zeta)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\setminus Q_{R})}.

Similarly,

\|\Pi(v)\|_{L^{2}}-\|\Pi(v\zeta)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\Pi(v(1-\zeta))\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|v(1-\zeta)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\setminus Q_{R})}.

Thus, we obtain the first inequality.

By the spectrum decomposition of
L_{n,k}
,
v\in\oplus_{\gamma\leq\Lambda}\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}
implies that,

|v(\theta,y)|\lesssim_{n,\Lambda}\|v\|_{L^{2}}\cdot(1+|y|^{2\Lambda+2}).

Therefore the second inequality follows from the first and

\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\setminus Q_{R})}^{2}\lesssim_{n,\Lambda}\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\backslash Q_{R}}(1+|y|^{2\Lambda+2})e^{-\frac{|X|^{2}}{4}}dX\lesssim_{n,\Lambda}\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\cdot e^{-R^{2}/5},

where the last inequality uses Proposition
2.4
with
\delta=1
.
∎

2.4.
Use of constants

Throughout this paper, we shall use the letter
C
to denote a constant that is allowed to vary from line to line (or even within the same line); we shall stress the functional dependence of any such constant on geometric quantities by including them in brackets, writing things like
C=C(n,\varepsilon)
.
We shall also use
\Psi(\varepsilon|C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{l})
to denote a constant depending on
\varepsilon,C_{1},\dots,C_{l}
and tending to
0
when
C_{1},\dots,C_{l}
are fixed and
\varepsilon\to 0
.
To save notation, we also write
A\sim_{a,b,\dots}A^{\prime}
(resp.
A\lesssim_{a,b,\dots}A^{\prime}
,
A\gtrsim_{a,b,\dots}A^{\prime}
) if for some constant
C(a,b,\dots)>1
,

C(a,b,\dots)^{-1}A^{\prime}\leq A\leq C(a,b,\dots)A^{\prime}\,;\qquad(\,\text{resp. }\ A\leq C(a,b,\dots)A^{\prime};\quad A\geq C(a,b,\dots)^{-1}A^{\prime}.\,)

3.
An
L^{2}
-nonconcentration for a general convex cylinder

In
[
SWX25
]
, the authors proved a general monotonicity for the
L^{2}
-distance to a certain level set flow. As an application, an
L^{2}
-nonconcentration for RMCF relative to the round cylinder was obtained in
[
SWX25
, Corollary 3.3]
. In this section, we generalize such a nonconcentration property relative to a general convex cylindrical flow. The consequence of it, which will be discussed in this section, is almost the same as those discussed in
[
SWX25
, Section 3]
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
1\leq k\leq n-1
be integers. We shall fix the following data
(\Omega,\mathbf{u},\beta,\rho)
such that,

\mathbf{(T{{1}})}

\Omega=\Omega_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
\Omega_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-k+1}
is a smooth strictly convex subset;

\mathbf{(T{{2}})}

\mathbf{u}(x,y):=\mathbf{u}_{\circ}(x):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbf{u}_{\circ}:\Omega_{\circ}\to\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
is the arrival-time-function of the MCF starting from
\partial\Omega_{\circ}
;
\beta\in(0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{\circ}(\mathbf{0})=0\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{\circ}|_{\partial\Omega_{\circ}}=-4\,,

\displaystyle\beta I_{n-k+1}\leq-(n-k)\cdot\nabla^{2}\mathbf{u}_{\circ}\leq\beta^{-1}I_{n-k+1}\,.

\mathbf{(T{{3}})}

\rho(X,t):=(-4\pi t)^{-n/2}\exp(|X|^{2}/4t)
. We denote for later reference
d\mu:=\rho(X,-1)dX
.

Remark 3.1
.

If
(\Omega,\mathbf{u},\beta)
satisfies

(
T1
)

and

(
T2
)

above, then one can directly check that for every
\lambda\geq 1
,
(\{\mathbf{u}^{(\lambda)}\geq-4\},\mathbf{u}^{(\lambda)},\beta)
also satisfies

(
T1
)

and

(
T2
)

, where

\mathbf{u}^{(\lambda)}(X):=\lambda^{2}\mathbf{u}(\lambda^{-1}X)\,.

Also, the RMCF associated to
\mathbf{u}
is

(3.1)

\displaystyle\tau\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau):=e^{\frac{\tau}{2}}\{\mathbf{u}=-e^{-\tau}\}\,.

Define the graphical function of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau)
over
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
as
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)
. Then it’s easy to check that

\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{(\lambda)}}(\cdot,\tau)=\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau+2\ln\lambda)\,.

Also, assumption

(
T2
)

implies that as
\tau\to+\infty
,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau)
converges to
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
. Then by Brakke-White’s
\varepsilon
-regularity
[
Whi05
]
,
\|\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{4}}\to 0
as
\tau\to+\infty
.

Remark 3.2
.

One explicit example that has already been discussed in
[
SWX25
, Section 3.2]
, is
\Omega_{\circ}=B^{n-k+1}(0,\sqrt{8(n-k)})
and

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}(x,y)=\mathbf{U}_{n,k}(x,y):=-\frac{|x|^{2}}{2(n-k)}\,,

which describes the generalized shrinking cylinder:

\mathbf{C}_{n,k}:=\coprod_{t\leq 0}\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:\mathbf{U}_{n,k}(x,y)=t\}\times\{t\}=\coprod_{t\leq 0}(\sqrt{-t}\,\mathcal{C}_{n,k})\times\{t\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}\,.

In this case, one can choose
\beta=1
. Another family of examples are the “low spherical flows”, to be constructed and discussed in Section
3.2
.

3.1.
Application of
L^{2}
-monotonicity formula

Let us fix a non-decreasing odd function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that

•

\chi^{\prime\prime}\leq 0
on
[0,+\infty)
;

•

\chi(s)=s
for
|s|\leq 1/2
,
\chi(s)=\operatorname{sgn}(s)
for
|s|\geq 1
.

Suppose
(\Omega,\mathbf{u},\beta,\rho)
satisfies

(
T1
)

-

(
T3
)

, and in addition

(3.3)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{4},\qquad\forall\,\tau\geq 0\,.

We define

(3.4)

\displaystyle\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau):=\chi\left(|x|-\varrho-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\varrho|x|^{-1}x)\right)\,,

which is a cut-off and regularization of the radial signed distance function to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}
, and define

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tilde{X},\tau)=\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tau):=f_{\circ}\left(\mathbf{u}_{\circ}(e^{-\tau/2}x)+e^{-\tau}\right)\,.

where
f_{\circ}
is specified in
[
SWX25
, Section 3,
(S2)
]
with
T_{\circ}=1
.
Then it’s easy to check that for every
(\tilde{X},\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

(3.6)

\displaystyle e^{-2\tau}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tilde{X},\tau)^{2}\lesssim_{n}\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tilde{X},\tau)^{2}\lesssim_{n}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tilde{X},\tau)^{2}\,.

This leads to the following non-concentration near infinity for rescaled mean curvature flow.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Omega,\mathbf{u},\beta,\rho
be satisfying

(
T1
)

-

(
T3
)

and (
3.3
). Then there exist
K=K(\beta,n)>2
with the following property. Let
T_{\pm}\geq 0
,
[T_{-},T_{+}]\ni\tau\to\mathcal{M}(\tau)
be a RMCF with finite entropy in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Then for every
T_{-}<\tau\leq T_{+}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}(\tau)}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}(1+(\tau-T_{-})|X|^{2})\ d\mu\lesssim_{n,\beta}\ e^{K(\tau-T_{-})}\int_{\mathcal{M}(T_{-})}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}\ d\mu\,.

Proof.

By Remark
3.1
, we can assume WLOG that
T_{-}=0
. If we let
\eta\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R})
be a non-negative function such that
\{\eta>0\}=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times I
for some interval
I
,
T_{\circ}=1
and
f_{\circ}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be chosen as in
[
SWX25
,
(S2)
]
, then it’s easy to check that
(S1)
-
(S4)
in
[
SWX25
, Section 3]
holds for
(\eta,\Omega,\mathbf{u},\beta^{\prime},\rho)
with
\beta^{\prime}>0
being some constant depending only on
n,\beta
.

Let
t\mapsto\mathbf{M}(t):=\sqrt{-t}\mathcal{M}(-\ln(-t))
be the MCF associated to
\mathcal{M}
. When taking
\eta\equiv 1
,
[
SWX25
, (3.4)]
applied to
\mathbf{M}
becomes

(3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left[(-t)^{2K}\int_{\mathbf{M}(t)}F^{2}\rho\ dX\right]+(-t)^{2K}\int_{\mathbf{M}(t)}\frac{c(n,\beta)|X|^{2}}{t^{2}}F^{2}\rho\ dX\leq 0

When taking
\eta(X,t)=\sqrt{1-|X|^{2}/t\,}\xi(t)
, where
\xi\in C_{c}^{2}(-1,0)
,
[
SWX25
, (3.4)]
implies,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}&\left[(-t)^{2K}\int_{\mathbf{M}(t)}F^{2}\cdot(1-|X|^{2}/t)\,\xi(t)^{2}\rho\ dX\right]\\
\leq\;&(-t)^{2K}\int_{\mathbf{M}(t)}\left[\left(-\frac{|X|^{2}}{t^{2}}-\frac{2n+8}{t}\right)\xi^{2}+\left(1-\frac{|X|^{2}}{t}\right)|\partial_{t}(\xi^{2})|\right]F^{2}\rho\ dX\end{split}

Now we rewrite everything under RMCF
\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
parametrized by
(\tilde{X},\tau)
using the change of variable
(X,t)=(e^{-\tau/2}\tilde{X},-e^{-\tau})
. Recall
\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}
is defined in (
3.5
). Then (
3.7
) is equivalent to,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\tau}\left[e^{-2K\tau}\int_{\mathcal{M}(\tau)}\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}\ d\mu\right]+C(n,\beta)e^{-2K\tau}\int_{\mathcal{M}(\tau)}\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}|X|^{2}\ d\mu\leq 0\,.\end{split}

And let
\tilde{\xi}(\tau):=\xi(-e^{-\tau})
, then (
3.8
) is equivalent to,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\tau}&\left[\,e^{-2K\tau}\int_{\mathcal{M}(\tau)}\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}(1+|X|^{2})\tilde{\xi}(\tau)^{2}\ d\mu\right]\\
\leq&\;e^{-2K\tau}\int_{\mathcal{M}(\tau)}\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}\left[(2n+8-|X|^{2})\tilde{\xi}(\tau)^{2}+(1+|X|^{2})\left|(\tilde{\xi}(\tau)^{2})^{\prime}\right|\right]\ d\mu\,.\end{split}

Integrate (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) with
\tilde{\xi}(s)=s/\tau
and use (
3.6
) proves the Lemma.
∎

3.2.
Low spherical flows

We shall construct a new family of arrival time functions satisfying

(
T1
)

,

(
T2
)

and (
3.3
).
Recall that
\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}
is specified in
(v)
of Section
2.3.3
, and
\gamma_{n,k}=1/(n-k)
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
(n-k)\geq 2
. There exists
\kappa_{\ref{Lem_L^2Noncon_LowSphericalMode}}(n)\in(0,1/4)
, a subset
\mathscr{U}
of the space of convex
C^{2}
-arrival time functions on some domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and a bijection map from
\kappa_{\ref{Lem_L^2Noncon_LowSphericalMode}}(n)
-ball in
\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}
to
\mathscr{U}
, such that,

(1)

for every
\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{G}_{n,k}
(the stabilizer of
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
, see its definition in (
2.5
)) and every
\psi\in\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}
with image
\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U}
, we have
\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{B}\in\mathscr{U}
is the image of
\psi\circ\mathbf{B}
;

(2)

there exists
\beta=\beta(n)>0
such that every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U}
satisfies the conditions

(
T1
)

&

(
T2
)

;

(3)

the image of
0\in\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}
is just the arrival time
\mathbf{U}_{n,k}
of the round cylinder; for any images
\mathbf{u}_{1},\mathbf{u}_{2}\in\mathscr{U}
of
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}
respectively and any
\tau\geq 0
,

\left\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}_{1}}(\cdot,\tau)-e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}\psi_{1})-(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}_{2}}(\cdot,\tau)-e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}\psi_{2})\right\|_{C^{4}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})}\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma_{n,k}\tau}\|\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})}\,.

In particular,
\|\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{4}}\leq\frac{1}{4}e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}
for every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U}
.

The key part of the proof is to construct convex RMCF with given asymptotics on the sphere. This is done is Appendix
A
.

Definition 3.5
.

We define
\mathscr{U}
to be a specific family of arrival time functions as:

•

\mathscr{U}:=\{\mathbf{U}_{n,k}\}
, if
n-k=1
;

•

\mathscr{U}
is given by Lemma
3.4
, if
n-k\geq 2
.

Elements in
\mathscr{U}
, after a possible parabolic dilation, are called
low spherical flows
asymptotic to
\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
.
We also define

(3.11)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathscr{U}}:=\{\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{B}:\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U},\ \mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{O}(n+1)\}\,.

Note that by Lemma
3.4

(1)
,
\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{B}\in\mathscr{U}
if and only if
\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{G}_{n,k}
.

Corollary 3.6
.

For every
\gamma\geq-1
, there exists
\Lambda_{\ref{Cor_L^2 Mono_Low Bd Upsilon_(u,u'; w)}}(n,\gamma)>2n
such that if
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{U}
are low spherical flows and
w\in(\oplus_{\lambda\leq\gamma}\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}))\cap\mathrm{W}_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
is a linear combination of eigenfunctions of
-L_{n,k}
. Then for every
R\geq\Lambda(n,\gamma)
and every
\tau\geq 0
, we have

\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}})(\cdot,\tau)+w\|_{L^{2}(Q_{R})}\gtrsim_{n}\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}})(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}+\|w\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Proof.

We assume the leading eigenmodes of
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}
and
\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}
are given by
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
respectively. By the item
(3)
of Lemma
3.4
,

\displaystyle\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}})(\cdot,\tau)+w\|_{L^{2}(Q_{R})}

\displaystyle\geq\|e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}(\psi-\psi^{\prime})+w\|_{L^{2}(Q_{R})}-\frac{1}{2}\|e^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma_{n,k}\tau}(\psi-\psi^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\|e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}(\psi-\psi^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim_{n}\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}})(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}(Q_{R})}.

By the orthogonality,

\|e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}(\psi-\psi^{\prime})+w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=e^{-2\gamma_{n,k}\tau}\|\psi-\psi^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\sim_{n}\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}})(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

Thus, it suffices to show that for
R\geq\Lambda(n,\gamma)
,

\|e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}(\psi-\psi^{\prime})+w\|_{L^{2}(Q_{R})}\geq\frac{3}{4}\|e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}(\psi-\psi^{\prime})+w\|_{L^{2}}\,.

By the expression of the eigenfunctions in
\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})
in Section
2.3.3
, for any unit eigenfunction
\phi
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, we have
|\phi(\theta,y)|\leq C_{\lambda}(1+|y|^{2|\lambda|+2})
. Therefore, if
v=\sum_{i=1}^{M}a_{i}\phi_{i}\in(\oplus_{\lambda\leq\gamma}\mathrm{W}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}))
is a Fourier decomposition of the eigenfunctions with eigenvalues
\leq\gamma
, we have

|v(\theta,y)|^{2}\lesssim_{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}|a_{i}|^{2}|\phi_{i}(\theta,y)|^{2}\lesssim_{\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^{M}|a_{i}|^{2}(1+|y|^{2|\gamma|+2})\|\phi_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\sim_{\gamma}(1+|y|^{2|\gamma|+2})\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

By taking
v=e^{-\gamma_{n,k}\tau}(\psi-\psi^{\prime})+w
and integrating
|v(\theta,y)|^{2}
outside
Q_{R}
, using Proposition
2.4
,

\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\backslash Q_{R})}\leq\Psi_{\mathcal{C}_{n,k},2|\gamma|+2}(R)^{1/2}\|v\|_{L^{2}}.

When
R
is sufficiently large, this implies that
\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k}\backslash Q_{R})}\leq 1/4\|v\|_{L^{2}}
, which implies that
\|v\|_{L^{2}(Q_{R})}\geq 3/4\|v\|_{L^{2}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.3.
Decay order and asymptotic rate

Recall that by Lemma
3.4
, every low spherical flow
\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U}
satisfies the conditions

(
T1
)

,

(
T2
)

and (
3.3
) for some
\beta=\beta(n)>0
. In particular, the
L^{2}
nonconcentration Lemma
3.3
applies to
\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}
. We shall discuss consequences of this
L^{2}
nonconcentration property in the rest of this section analogous to those in
[
SWX25
, Section 3]
. Although most lemma in this subsection work for general arrival time function satisfying

(
T1
)

,

(
T2
)

and (
3.3
), to avoid technicality, we restrict ourselves to the low spherical flows
\mathscr{U}
.

For every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}:=\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau)\}_{\tau\geq 0}
be the RMCF associated to
\mathbf{u}
as in Remark
3.1
. For a hypersurface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we define the
L^{2}
-distance from
\Sigma
to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau)
by,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\Sigma)^{2}:=\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}\ d\mu\,;

For later reference, we also introduce the distance to the rotated relevant flow. For
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}=\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{B}
with
\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U}
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathrm{O}(n+1)
, define

(3.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{\tilde{\mathbf{u}}}(\tau,\Sigma)^{2}:=\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{B}(X),\tau)^{2}\ d\mu\,;

Note that this is well-defined, since whenever
\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{O}(n+1)
such that
\mathbf{u}\circ\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{u}
, we must have
\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{C}_{n,k})=\mathcal{C}_{n,k}
and
\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{B}(X),\tau)^{2}=\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}
for every
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

The following Lemma comparing
L^{2}
-distance from translated hypersurfaces to possibly a different relative flow will be useful later.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a hypersurface with
\lambda[\Sigma]<+\infty
,
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{U}
. Then for every
\tau\geq 0
, we have

(3.14)

\displaystyle|\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}(\tau,\Sigma)-\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\Sigma)|\leq\lambda[\Sigma]^{1/2}\|\chi\|_{C^{1}}\cdot\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}})(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{0}}\,.

Also for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
|\mathbf{y}|\leq 1
, and every
R\geq 1
, we have,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\Sigma+(0,\mathbf{y}))\lesssim_{n}\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\Sigma)\cdot e^{R/4}+\lambda[\Sigma]^{1/2}\cdot e^{-R^{2}/64}\,.

Proof.

(
3.14
) follows by the triangle inequality of Gaussian
L^{2}
norm on
\mathcal{M}
and the pointwise estimate below from definition in (
3.4
),

\left|\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)-\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}(X,\tau)\right|\leq\|\chi\|_{C^{1}}\cdot\|(\varphi_{\mathbf{u}}-\varphi_{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}})(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{0}}\,.

To prove (
3.15
), note that for
|\mathbf{y}|\leq 1
and every
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle|X+(0,\mathbf{y})|^{2}\geq|X|^{2}-2|X|+1\geq\frac{1}{4}|X|^{2}-1\,.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\Sigma+(0,\mathbf{y}))^{2}

\displaystyle\sim_{n}\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X+(0,\mathbf{y}),\tau)^{2}\cdot e^{-|X+(0,\mathbf{y})|^{2}/4}\ dX

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\int_{\Sigma\cap Q_{R}}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}\cdot e^{R/2}\cdot e^{-|X|^{2}/4}\ dX+\int_{\Sigma\setminus Q_{R}}\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)^{2}\cdot e^{-|X|^{2}/16}\ dX

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\Sigma)^{2}\cdot e^{R/2}+\int_{2^{-1}\cdot(\Sigma\setminus Q_{R})}e^{-|X|^{2}/4}\ dX

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\Sigma)^{2}\cdot e^{R/2}+\lambda[\Sigma]\cdot e^{-R^{2}/32}\,;

where the second inequality follows from the translation invariance of
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
direction; the third inequality follows from change of variable and that
|\overline{\dist}_{\mathbf{u}}(X,\tau)|\leq 1
, and the last inequality follows from Proposition
2.4
.
This finishes the proof of (
3.15
).
∎

For
\mathbf{u}\in\tilde{\mathscr{U}}
and a RMCF
\tau\to\mathcal{M}(\tau)
with
\tau,\tau+1\in I
, we also define the
decay order
of
\mathcal{M}
at time
\tau
relative to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{u}}
by

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\mathcal{M}):=\ln\left(\frac{\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\mathcal{M}(\tau))}{\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau+1,\mathcal{M}(\tau+1))}\right)

Note that when
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{U}_{n,k}
, using the notation in
[
SWX25
, Section 3.3]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{n,k}(\mathcal{M}(\tau))=\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{U}_{n,k}}(\tau,\mathcal{M}(\tau))\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{n,k}(\tau,\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{U}_{n,k}}(\tau,\mathcal{M})\,.

Also note that by Lemma
3.3
, for every RMCF
\mathcal{M}
, we always have the uniform lower bound

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\mathcal{M})\geq-C(n)\,.

For
R>0
, recall that
Q_{R}:=B_{R}^{n-k+1}\times B_{R}^{k}
. We also define the decay order of a RMCF
\mathcal{M}
restricted in
Q_{R}
: if
\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap Q_{R},\mathcal{M}(\tau+1)\cap Q_{R}\neq\emptyset
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\mathcal{M}\cap Q_{R}):=\ln\left(\frac{\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap Q_{R})}{\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau+1,\mathcal{M}(\tau+1)\cap Q_{R})}\right)

Similar to
[
SWX25
, Corollary 3.4]
, we have the following error estimate on this localized decay order.

Corollary 3.8
.

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists
R_{\ref{Cor_L^2 Noncon_Compare d(M), cN(M) and d(M in Q_R), cN(M in Q_R)}}(\varepsilon,n)\gg 1
such that the following hold. If
T_{\circ}\geq 0
,
\mathbf{u}\in\mathscr{U}
,
\mathcal{M}
is a RMCF in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
over
[T_{\circ},T_{\circ}+2]
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{u}}(T_{\circ},\mathcal{M}),\;\;\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{u}}(T_{\circ}+1,\mathcal{M})\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\,.

Then
\forall\,\tau\in(T_{\circ},T_{\circ}+1]
,
\forall\,R\geq(\tau-T_{\circ})^{-1/2}R_{\ref{Cor_L^2 Noncon_Compare d(M), cN(M) and d(M in Q_R), cN(M in Q_R)}}
, we have
\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap Q_{R}\neq\emptyset,\ \mathcal{M}(\tau+1)\cap Q_{R}\neq\emptyset
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\mathcal{M}(\tau))^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{u}}(\tau,\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap Q_{R})
